     1	# AGENTS.md
     2	
     3	Repository-wide instructions for AI coding/writing agents (VS Code Copilot,
     4	Kimi-Code, OpenCode, and any other agent that reads this file).
     5	
     6	## Paper Writing Workflow (applies to ALL paper tasks)
     7	
     8	This repo tracks a pipeline of research papers. Any task that touches a paper —
     9	planning, writing, rewriting, reviewing, advising, figure generation, or
    10	submission checks — MUST follow the shared methodology:
    11	
    12	1. **Read** `paper-workflow/methodology.md` first. It defines the coarse-to-fine
    13	   workflow (Layer 0–5) and the **Rule 0 storyline gate**.
    14	2. **Read** the relevant per-paper config at `paper-workflow/papers/<name>.md`
    15	   (match by repo folder; see `paper-workflow/README.md`).
    16	3. **Obey the gate:** do not write/rewrite prose until the storyline (L3) is
    17	   confirmed for the target subsection. If unconfirmed, STOP and align with the
    18	   user first.
    19	4. **Quality-critical work is done by the main agent only.** No subagents for
    20	   writing, review, audit, or QC. Subagents only for mechanical tasks (compile,
    21	   git, figure scripting).
    22	5. **Run the de-AI pass** (`paper-workflow/de-ai-style.md`) on all newly written
    23	   or rewritten prose before considering it done.
    24	
    25	For review tasks, use `paper-workflow/reviewer-rubric.md`. For strategic
    26	decisions, use `paper-workflow/supervisor-lenses.md`. For venue requirements
    27	(CFP sourcing, reference corpus, pre-submission compliance gate), use
    28	`paper-workflow/venue-protocol.md`. For citation verification (existence,
    29	usage accuracy, bib hygiene), use `paper-workflow/citation-check.md` —
    30	mandatory after any bib edit and before submission. Before writing or
    31	rewriting a Related Work section, run
    32	`paper-workflow/related-work-research.md`.
    33	
    34	## Repo Structure Note
    35	
    36	This is a monorepo with several submodules (see `.gitmodules`). Before editing a
    37	submodule, confirm you are inside the intended repo. Do not commit unrelated
    38	submodule pointer changes unless asked.
    39	
    40	---
    41	
    42	# Workspace Rules (local)
    43	
    44	## Git push policy (per repository)
    45	
    46	- **`croqtile` (github.com:LancerLab/croqtile) — PR only.** NEVER push directly
    47	  to `main`. This applies to every checkout, including the nested
    48	  `croqtile/` submodules inside artifact repos (`mara-artifacts`,
    49	  `svn-artifacts`, `coir-artifact`, `croq-dsl-artifacts`) and the dev checkout
    50	  at `~/dev/choreo`. Workflow: create a feature branch, push the branch,
    51	  raise a pull request.
    52	- **Artifact repos under `github.com:gxf` (e.g. `mara-artifacts`)** — direct
    53	  pushes to `main` are allowed.
    54	- All other repos: never push unless the user explicitly approves it in the
    55	  current conversation (one-time approval per push).
    56	
    57	## Submodule discipline
    58	
    59	Artifact repos embed `croqtile/` as a nested submodule. When upstream croqtile
    60	work lands (via PR), update the submodule pin in the artifact repo and commit
    61	the pointer bump there.
    62	
    63	**Never create `AGENTS.md` (or any agent-rule files) inside a croqtile
    64	checkout/submodule.** Agent rules for croqtile work live in this workspace
    65	file only.
    66	
    67	## Submodule authentication (HTTPS + API token)
    68	
    69	All nested `croqtile/` submodules point at `https://github.com/LancerLab/croqtile.git`
    70	(a public repo), so artifact evaluation can clone with **no credentials**.
    71	
    72	To **push** over HTTPS (feature branch → PR), authenticate with the GitHub API
    73	token `GITHUB_TOKEN` — a PAT for `Garfee023` with `repo` scope, exported by
    74	`~/dev/script/garfee.sh`. Git over HTTPS uses Basic auth, so:
    75	
    76	- One-off push:
    77	  `git push https://Garfee023:$GITHUB_TOKEN@github.com/LancerLab/croqtile.git <branch>`
    78	- Persistent helper (works across environments):
    79	  `git config --global credential.helper '!f() { echo "username=Garfee023"; echo "password=$GITHUB_TOKEN"; }; f'`
    80	
    81	`GITHUB_TOKEN` only reaches `LancerLab/*` (and other `Garfee023` repos). The
    82	private `gfx/*` repos are **not** reachable by it — those use the SSH key
    83	`~/.ssh/id_ed25519_gitlab` (wired via the superproject's `core.sshCommand`).
    84	
    85	## Code style
    86

    88	  (repo `.clang-format`, LLVM style) before committing.
    89	- Commit messages: describe work in general concepts; keep it short and
    90	  accurate; no file/API-level enumeration.

     1	# Paper Methodology — Coarse-to-Fine Storyline Alignment
     2	
     3	> **Tool-agnostic core.** This file is the single source of truth for *how* any
     4	> paper in this repo is developed with an AI agent. It is written as imperative
     5	> rules so it executes identically in Copilot, Kimi-Code, OpenCode, or any
     6	> agent that can read Markdown. Load it via the root `AGENTS.md`.
     7	
     8	---
     9	
    10	## The One Rule (Rule 0 — Storyline Gate)
    11	
    12	**Before ANY paper writing action (drafting, rewriting, reviewing, advising),
    13	the agent MUST:**
    14	
    15	1. Read the paper's config at `paper-workflow/papers/<name>.md`.
    16	2. Check the `L3 confirmed` status for the target subsection in the paper's
    17	   `storyline-state.md` (or the config itself during early planning).
    18	3. **If L3 is NOT confirmed → STOP.** Do not write prose. Align the storyline
    19	   with the human first (work the layers below).
    20	4. **If the config/state file is missing → STOP.** Tell the user a storyline
    21	   alignment is needed before writing.
    22	
    23	**This gate is non-negotiable. No writing without a confirmed storyline.**
    24	
    25	The storyline is the *what* (what each paragraph says). Prose is the *how*.
    26	Getting the what wrong is always more expensive than rough prose.
    27	
    28	---
    29	
    30	## The 6-Layer Hierarchy
    31	
    32	Work **coarse-to-fine**. Each layer MUST be confirmed with the human before
    33	descending to the next. Never skip a layer.
    34	
    35	```
    36	Layer 0: Core claim + contributions (the "red thread")
    37	Layer 1: Section outline + page budget
    38	Layer 2: Subsection topics + C→M→E mapping
    39	Layer 3: Paragraph-level storyline (topic sentence per paragraph)
    40	Layer 4: Writing plan (evidence, form, length per paragraph)
    41	Layer 5: Implementation (actual prose)
    42	```
    43	
    44	Confirmation status is tracked in each paper's `storyline-state.md`
    45	(living document, one per paper).
    46	
    47	---
    48	
    49	## Layer 0 — Core Claim + Contributions
    50	
    51	**Goal:** agree on what the paper claims and what it contributes.
    52	
    53	**Protocol:**
    54	1. Read the paper config (`papers/<name>.md`) and any existing abstract / plan.
    55	2. Present to the user:
    56	   - The **one-sentence core claim**.
    57	   - The **contribution list** (numbered).
    58	   - A **C→M→E table**: each contribution → the methodology section that
    59	     delivers it → the evaluation result that supports it.
    60	3. Ask for confirm / reframe / add / remove.
    61	
    62	**Checkpoint format:**
    63	```
    64	Core claim: [one sentence]
    65	
    66	| # | Contribution | Methodology | Evaluation |
    67	|---|---|---|---|
    68	| C1 | ... | §X.Y | §Z.W (table/figure) |
    69	```
    70	
    71	**Red flags (flag, don't proceed silently):**
    72	- A contribution with no evaluation support → **gap**.
    73	- An evaluation result serving no contribution → **orphan**.
    74	- A methodology section serving no contribution → **dead weight**.
    75	
    76	---
    77	
    78	## Layer 1 — Section Outline + Page Budget
    79	
    80	**Goal:** agree on section structure and space allocation.
    81	
    82	**Protocol:**
    83	1. List each section with: title, label, target page count, one-sentence "job".
    84	2. Sum must fit the venue page limit (from `papers/<name>.md`, which must have
    85	   been filled from the **live CFP** via `venue-protocol.md` Part 1 — never
    86	   from memory).
    87	3. Present for confirmation.
    88	
    89	**Checkpoint format:**
    90	```
    91	| § | Title | Pages | Job |
    92	|---|-------|-------|-----|
    93	| 1 | Introduction | 1.5 | Expose problem → root cause → proposal → results |
    94	```
    95	
    96	**Red flags:**
    97	- Total exceeds page limit by >0.5 pages.
    98	- A section whose "job" serves no contribution.
    99	- A missing section the venue expects (e.g., no Related Work).
   100	
   101	---
   102	
   103	## Layer 2 — Subsection Topics + C→M→E Mapping
   104	
   105	**Goal:** for each section, define subsections and their "job".
   106	
   107	**Protocol:** per section, list subsections with: title, ~paragraphs, "job"
   108	(what question it answers), which contribution it serves. Verify methodology
   109	subsections have matching evaluation support. Confirm with user.
   110	
   111	**Red flags:**
   112	- Subsection with no clear job.
   113	- Two subsections overlapping in scope.
   114	- A contribution with no subsection serving it.
   115	
   116	---
   117	
   118	## Layer 3 — Paragraph-Level Storyline
   119	
   120	**Goal:** for each subsection, define what each paragraph says (topic-sentence
   121	level). **This is the most important layer.**
   122	
   123	**Protocol** (one section at a time; user picks which next):
   124	For each paragraph, record:
   125	- **Topic sentence** — the claim/point.
   126	- **Reader knows** — what they've learned up to here.
   127	- **Why here** — why this position, not earlier/later.
   128	- **Key evidence** — data/citation backing it.
   129	- **→ leads to** — what comes next and why the reader is now ready.
   130	
   131	Confirm with user, then mark the subsection `L3 confirmed` in state.
   132	
   133	---
   134	
   135	## Layer 4 — Writing Plan
   136	
   137	For each confirmed paragraph, produce a plan:
   138	
   139	| Field | Content |
   140	|---|---|
   141	| Topic sentence | from L3 |
   142	| Evidence | exact figure/table/citation/lemma |
   143	| Form | example, contrast, definition, argument |
   144	| Length | sentences / words |
   145	
   146	Present a batch for approval before writing.
   147	
   148	---
   149	
   150	## Layer 5 — Implementation
   151	
   152	Write the actual prose into the paper source (LaTeX/Markdown), one subsection
   153	at a time. After writing, run the QC rounds in `academic-writing` flow (see
   154	`methodology.md` "Quality gates" below) and mark the subsection `qc-pass`.
   155	
   156	---
   157	
   158	## Quality Gates (after L5, before review)
   159	
   160	Four rounds, in order:
   161	
   162	1. **Structure** — every paragraph does its L3 job; nothing added/removed vs
   163	   the confirmed storyline.
   164	2. **Numbers** — every claim is backed by data/citation; cross-check all
   165	   numbers against the evidence sources. No fabricated or drifted numbers.
   166	3. **De-AI** — run the `de-ai-style.md` pass on touched paragraphs.
   167	4. **Citations** — run `citation-check.md` (Pass 1 minimum after any bib edit;
   168	   all three passes before submission).
   169	
   170	## Submission Gates (when the draft is a submission candidate)
   171	
   172	1. **Venue compliance** — run `venue-protocol.md` Part 3 (template, page
   173	   limit on the built PDF, anonymity, fonts, disclosures, logistics) and
   174	   record results in the paper's `plan/venue-compliance.md`.
   175	2. **CFP re-verification** — re-fetch the live CFP page within the last week
   176	   before the deadline (`venue-protocol.md` Part 1, step 4).
   177	3. **Full citation check** — `citation-check.md`, all three passes, clean
   178	   record in `plan/review-state.md`.
   179	
   180	---
   181	
   182	## Division of Labor (who does what)
   183	
   184	- **Human (owner):** Layer 0–3 decisions — the storyline, claims, contributions,
   185	  design judgment. The agent *presents options and asks*; it does not decide.
   186	- **Agent (labor):** Layer 4–5 execution, number cross-checks, de-AI passes,
   187	  mechanical tasks (compile, git, figure generation).
   188	
   189	**HARD RULE — no subagents for quality-critical work.** Review, audit, QC, and
   190	paragraph writing are done by the main agent directly. Subagents lose storyline
   191	context and produce drift. Subagents are allowed only for mechanical tasks
   192	(compile, git, figure scripting).
   193	
   194	---
   195	
   196	## Paper Types and the Evaluation Leaf
   197	
   198	Layer 0–2's "evaluation" column differs by paper type. Apply the right one:
   199	
   200	| Paper type | "Evaluation" leaf | Reviewer lens |
   201	|---|---|---|
   202	| Empirical systems (SALA, MARA, CoIR, SVN, SC) | Benchmarks, baselines, ablations, variance | systems/HPC reviewer |
   203	| Theory / formal (OOPSLA type system) | Theorems, lemmas, mechanization status | PL/type-theory + proof-assistant expert |
   204	
   205	When seeding a paper config, record which type it is so the review and
   206	supervisor lenses auto-adapt.


</details>

    89	\begin{document}
    90	
    91	\title{The Compiler as Auditor: Safety Ledgers for AI-Native GPU Kernels}
    92	
    93	%% Anonymous authors for submission
    94	\author{Anonymous Author(s)}
    95	\affiliation{%
    96	  \institution{Anonymous Institution}
    97	  \city{Anonymous City}
    98	  \country{Anonymous Country}
    99	}
   100	\email{anonymous@example.com}
   101	
   102	\begin{abstract}
   103	AI code generators now produce GPU kernels at a pace no human reviews, and
   104	shape-related defects --- dimension mismatches, out-of-bounds accesses, DMA
   105	misconfiguration --- are their dominant class of silent failures. Existing
   106	answers are unsatisfying: runtime sanitizers are costly and incomplete,
   107	external verifiers cannot see kernel semantics, and compiler assertion
   108	facilities check only what programmers remember to write.
   109	
   110	We present a different contract: the compiler as auditor. In the \sys kernel
   111	compiler, every safety obligation is \emph{generated from the semantics of
   112	kernel constructs}---element accesses and chunked tile indices generate
   113	bounds obligations, DMA transfers generate shape-compatibility obligations,
   114	and target-specific operations generate hardware-constraint obligations---and every obligation receives exactly one of three audited
   115	outcomes: \emph{proven} at compile time, \emph{refuted} as a compile-time bug
   116	report, or \emph{materialized} as a runtime check under an explicit cost
   117	budget. Nothing is silently dropped. The discharge engine is not a bolted-on
   118	analysis: it is the compiler's own shape inference, made assessment-complete by
   119	bounded types for parallel iterators and interval reasoning over them.
   120	
   121	On a 310-case suite spanning the 15 operator categories that dominate GPU and
   122	machine-learning workloads, 93.2\% of 17{,}717 generated obligations are
   123	discharged statically (99.2\% on static-shape cases, 87.9\% on dynamic-shape
   124	ones). The residual obligations become lightweight runtime checks under an
   125	explicit cost budget, and the two stages together stop all 210 injected shape
   126	bugs before any device execution --- each refuted at compile time. An
   127	information-dependence audit shows that
   128	every symbolic discharge depends on block/thread-parallel structure that
   129	lowering destroys. The runtime checks add negligible overhead under the default
   130	cost budget.
   131	\end{abstract}
   132

   156	%% ----------------------------------------------------------------
   157	\section{Introduction}
   158	\label{sec:intro}
   159	
   160	GPU kernels are increasingly written by machines. LLM-based code generators
   161	and autonomous tuning agents now produce tile-level kernel variants at a rate
   162	no human reviewer can
   163	match~\cite{tillet2019triton,ouyang2025kernelbench}. The verification side has
   164	not kept pace: generated kernels routinely contain subtle correctness bugs, and
   165	manual verification --- reliable as it is --- cannot scale to match LLM output
   166	rates, creating a critical validation
   167	bottleneck~\cite{chatterjee2025proofwright}. This bottleneck is what keeps AI
   168	kernel generation from becoming a stable, trusted process: a generator that
   169	emits candidates faster than they can be checked must either be trusted blindly
   170	or slowed to a human pace.
   171	
   172	The failures that jam this bottleneck are severe and hard to trace. Kernel
   173	generation is usually framed as a performance problem, but the obstacle that
   174	actually stalls it is \emph{silently wrong} code: a violated condition more
   175	often returns a plausible-looking wrong tensor than an error, surfacing only
   176	far downstream of its cause. A kernel that tiles a dynamic dimension but drops
   177	its boundary mask passes every test whose size divides the tile, and reads past
   178	the buffer on the first mismatched input (\S\ref{sec:problem})---a silent
   179	failure that escapes every exercised input and surfaces far from the bug.
   180	Recent empirical work names these failures: out-of-bounds element
   181	accesses, dimension mismatches, and DMA misconfigurations dominate CUDA and
   182	tile-program bug studies~\cite{wu2019cuda,rathnasuriya2026tilebugs}; the same
   183	silent shape and memory faults recur in deep-learning
   184	frameworks~\cite{islam2019dlbugs,humbatova2020taxonomy} and in the compilers
   185	that lower these kernels~\cite{li2026silence}.
   186	
   187	Today's answers leave a gap between \emph{checking} and \emph{accounting}.
   188	Runtime sanitizers (e.g., compute-sanitizer~\cite{nvidia2024sanitizer}) catch
   189	violations after the fact, at the price of execution overhead and only on
   190	exercised inputs. External verifiers operate post-hoc on generated CUDA, below
   191	the abstraction level where kernel intent lives. And the testing-based
   192	validation these pipelines actually rely on is unsound: fixed-shape allclose
   193	oracles certify silently-wrong kernels~\cite{sarkar2026correctness}, and an
   194	adversarial audit of 2{,}638 harness-accepted LLM kernels found 39.5\% of them
   195	broken beyond any tolerance
   196	argument~\cite{shah2026contract}. Compiler
   197	assertion facilities check what the programmer remembered to
   198	write~\cite{bodik2000abcd} --- and an
   199	AI generator remembers nothing. What is missing is a \emph{complete} account:
   200	every way a kernel can be unsafe, enumerated, and each one demonstrably
   201	discharged, refuted, or paid for at runtime. A compiler that provides such an
   202	account removes the bottleneck: the safer the compiler, the more autonomy the
   203	generator can be granted.
   204	
   205	This paper recasts the kernel compiler as an \emph{auditor}. In the \sys
   206	compiler, safety obligations are not annotations
   207	supplied by the programmer;
   208	they are \emph{generated from the semantics of the kernel constructs}
   209	themselves: an element access or chunked index generates a bounds
   210	obligation, a DMA transfer or multi-operand operation generates a
   211	shape-compatibility obligation, a loop generates an iteration-validity
   212	obligation, and a target-specific operation generates a
   213	hardware-constraint obligation. Because generation is driven by the constructs,
   214	the obligation set is complete by construction. Every obligation then receives
   215	exactly one of three audited outcomes: \emph{proven} at compile time,
   216	\emph{refuted} as a compile-time bug report, or \emph{materialized} as a
   217	runtime check under an explicit cost budget. The per-kernel \ledger{} --- a
   218	machine-readable record of every obligation and its outcome --- makes this
   219	accounting inspectable.
   220	
   221	This discharge is deliberately cheap. Obligations are resolved by the
   222	compiler's own shape-inference machinery, extended with one domain-specific
   223	ingredient --- \emph{bounded types} for parallel iterators (the block index
   224	$p$ in $\texttt{parallel}\ p\ \texttt{by}\ N$ has type $\mathsf{BInt}[0,N)$)
   225	--- and settled by two classical evaluations over the inferred shapes:
   226	constant folding of ground predicates and interval reasoning over bounded
   227	types. Discharge happens at the stage where the obligation's semantics is
   228	fixed --- the semantic stage for shape and bounds, target selection for
   229	hardware constraints --- so the symbolic proofs run while parallel structure
   230	is still present as type-level facts; after lowering, those facts dissolve
   231	into flat index arithmetic and the same proofs become impossible
   232	(\S\ref{subsec:rq6}). The auditor therefore adds no new analysis phase --- it
   233	is the compiler keeping promises it already computes --- and its cost is
   234	negligible: 0.6\% median compile-time overhead (\S\ref{subsec:rq4}).
   235	
   236	On a benchmark of 310 tile kernels spanning 15 operator categories --- 159 of
   237	them carrying symbolic dimensions --- \sys generates 17{,}717 safety
   238	obligations and discharges 93.2\% statically (99.2\% static-shape, 87.9\%
   239	dynamic-shape), while every safety violation surfaces before any device
   240	execution: all 210 injected shape bugs are caught --- each refuted at compile
   241	time --- versus 139 and 80 detected by MLIR and IREE pipelines
   242	(\S\ref{subsec:rq2}). The residual
   243	runtime checks add no measurable overhead (\S\ref{subsec:rq3}), so the
   244	safety comes essentially for free.
   245	
   246	\paragraph{Contributions.}
   247	\begin{itemize}
   248	  \item \textbf{The safety-ledger discipline} (\S\ref{sec:ledger}): safety
   249	    obligations generated from kernel-construct and target semantics, with
   250	    total accounting --- every obligation is proven, refuted, or
   251	    cost-budgeted; the accounting identity is machine-checkable per kernel.
   252	  \item \textbf{Assessment-complete shape inference}
   253	    (\S\ref{subsec:resolution}): bounded types for parallel iterators and
   254	    interval reasoning over them, with a bounded-interval soundness lemma,
   255	    making safety discharge a byproduct of the compiler's own shape inference.
   256	  \item \textbf{Cost-gated residual materialization}
   257	    (\S\ref{subsec:materialization}): assertion hoisting under a cost model
   258	    with structured diagnostics.
   259	  \item \textbf{Empirical demonstration} (\S\ref{sec:eval}): 93.2\% static
   260	    discharge over 17{,}717 obligations on 310 kernels, detection of all 210
   261	    injected bugs before device execution, a minimality ablation, and an
   262	    information-dependence audit
   263	    showing that all symbolic discharge depends on parallel structure that
   264	    lowering destroys.
   265	\end{itemize}
   266	
   267	%% ----------------------------------------------------------------
   268	\section{Background: Safety Obligations and Where They Get Lost}
   269	\label{sec:problem}
   270	
   271	%% The workflow figure is a full-width float. LaTeX places `figure*` only at
   272	%% the top of the page AFTER the one on which it is declared; declaring it at
   273	%% the head of §2 (page 2) lands it at the top of the page where §3 opens
   274	%% (page 3), numbered ahead of the single-column code listing in §3.
   275	\input{figures/fig_workflow}
   276	
   277	GPUs are programmed as parallel computations over tiles. A kernel is written
   278	not as one thread's worth of work but as a computation over a grid of blocks
   279	and a tile of elements, every tensor element reached by indices computed from
   280	block, thread, and loop coordinates. This model earns its speed from exactly
   281	the mechanisms that demand explicit reasoning about shapes and indices ---
   282	tiling, shared-memory staging, DMA, and tensor-core calls --- and so it
   283	concentrates safety risk in those same constructs: the code that makes a
   284	kernel fast is the code that can read past a buffer or pair tensors of
   285	mismatched shape. The conditions these constructs impose are not optional
   286	annotations; they are implied by the constructs themselves, whether or not
   287	anyone records them.
   288	
   289	\paragraph{Safety obligations.}
   290	We call these conditions \emph{safety obligations}. They matter because they
   291	are where GPU and machine-learning programming time actually goes, and they
   292	are silent: a violated obligation more often returns a plausible-looking
   293	wrong tensor than a crash. Rather than treat these obligations as an
   294	afterthought of testing or a burden on the programmer, this paper makes them
   295	first-class---generating every obligation from the constructs that imply it
   296	and settling each one, explicitly, before execution.
   297	
   298	The obligations themselves fall into a small set of classes. An element
   299	access \texttt{a.at(i)} is defined only when $0 \le i < \mathit{dim}(a)$, and
   300	a chunked tile index $\texttt{p\#n}$ inherits a range from its iterator ---
   301	the \emph{element-access} class, the out-of-bounds faults that dominate
   302	empirical CUDA bug studies~\cite{wu2019cuda} and that post-hoc tools such as
   303	SCuBA must reconstruct to find after lowering~\cite{ghosh2026scuba}. A DMA
   304	transfer or a multi-operand operation requires source and destination shapes
   305	to agree --- the \emph{shape-compatibility} class, the tensor shape faults
   306	that Tensfa repairs~\cite{wu2021tensfa} and the data bugs of deep-learning
   307	studies~\cite{islam2019dlbugs}. A parallel loop requires a non-empty iteration
   308	space and a nonzero stride --- the \emph{iteration-validity} class. A
   309	tensor-core operation imposes hardware alignment and shape/divisibility
   310	constraints --- the \emph{hardware-constraint} class, documented empirically
   311	by device-specific tile-programming bugs such as WGMMA shape
   312	divisibility~\cite{rathnasuriya2026tilebugs}. Obligations exist
   313	whether or not anyone writes them down, and the questions a toolchain must
   314	answer are the same across all four classes: which obligations are checked,
   315	where are they settled, and who keeps count.
   316	
   317	\paragraph{A concrete failure.}
   318	\label{sec:painpoint}
   319	The matrix-multiplication kernel of Figure~\ref{fig:running} is not exotic; it
   320	is written every day in Triton, the de-facto tile-level DSL. Consider its
   321	Triton form, which tiles the $M \times K$ by $K \times N$ product into
   322	\texttt{BLOCK\_M} $\times$ \texttt{BLOCK\_N} accumulators:
   323	
   324	\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python]
   325	@triton.jit
   326	def matmul(lhs, rhs, out, M, N, K,
   327	           BLOCK_M: tl.constexpr, BLOCK_N: tl.constexpr,
   328	           BLOCK_K: tl.constexpr):
   329	    pid_m = tl.program_id(0)
   330	    pid_n = tl.program_id(1)
   331	    rm = pid_m * BLOCK_M + tl.arange(0, BLOCK_M)
   332	    rn = pid_n * BLOCK_N + tl.arange(0, BLOCK_N)
   333	    rk = tl.arange(0, BLOCK_K)
   334	    # BUG: boundary masks rm < M and rn < N are missing
   335	    acc = tl.zeros((BLOCK_M, BLOCK_N), dtype=tl.float32)
   336	    for k in range(0, K, BLOCK_K):
   337	        a = tl.load(lhs + rm[:, None] * K
   338	                    + (k + rk)[None, :])
   339	        b = tl.load(rhs + (k + rk)[:, None] * N
   340	                    + rn[None, :])
   341	        acc += tl.dot(a, b)
   342	    tl.store(out + rm[:, None] * N + rn[None, :], acc)
   343	\end{lstlisting}
   344	
   345	The kernel is correct on every input whose $M$ and $N$ are multiples of
   346	\texttt{BLOCK\_M} and \texttt{BLOCK\_N}, and silently wrong otherwise: on the
   347	last tile, the \texttt{rm} entries that run past $M$ make \texttt{tl.load}
   348	read adjacent memory and \texttt{tl.store} overwrite adjacent rows, both
   349	without a crash and both producing a plausible tensor. Nothing in Triton
   350	forces the mask --- it is a programmer convention, not a generated obligation
   351	--- so the omission passes review and passes every test sized to divide the
   352	tile. The defect is an \emph{element-access} violation whose guard depends on
   353	a dynamic extent (the symbolic \texttt{M} of Figure~\ref{fig:running}) --- the
   354	out-of-bounds faults that dominate empirical CUDA and tile-program bug
   355	studies~\cite{wu2019cuda,rathnasuriya2026tilebugs}.
   356	
   357	A second, independent silent failure sits one signature away: nothing ties the
   358	contraction extents of the two operands together, so a \texttt{rhs} whose
   359	first dimension disagrees with \texttt{K} makes the loop read past the end of
   360	one operand and fold garbage into every accumulator. That is a
   361	\emph{shape-compatibility} obligation --- the very contract that the symbolic
   362	\texttt{K} of Figure~\ref{fig:running} names --- and it is no more optional
   363	than the mask.
   364	
   365	\paragraph{Current practice and its limits.}
   366	To see why the mask is optional rather than obligatory, follow the kernel
   367	through Triton's pipeline. At the front end the tensor-level intent is
   368	intact: \texttt{lhs} is an $M \times K$ tensor, \texttt{rm} is a
   369	$\texttt{BLOCK\_M}$-wide tile index, and \texttt{tl.load} is valid only when
   370	every \texttt{rm} entry lies below $M$. But that validity condition is
   371	never materialized as an obligation. The \texttt{mask=rm < M} that would
   372	guard a boundary tile is a value the programmer writes and passes as an
   373	operand; Triton treats it as data to lower, not as a condition to verify. If
   374	the programmer omits it, as in the listing, there is no check to skip --- the
   375	condition never exists in the IR, so no later pass can rediscover it.
   376	
   377	The lowering then erases the structure that would settle it anyway. Triton
   378	lowers the \texttt{tt} dialect through TritonGPU (\texttt{ttg}) to the LLVM
   379	dialect and on to PTX, and each step discards exactly the information a bound
   380	check would need. \texttt{ttg} annotates tensors with \emph{layouts} --- how
   381	elements are spread across warps and lanes --- but a layout records where an
   382	element lives, not whether it lies inside the tensor. By the LLVM dialect the
   383	parallel tile has become per-thread scalar pointer arithmetic, and the
   384	relation between \texttt{rm} and $M$ has dissolved into flat addresses. A
   385	downstream analysis therefore sees raw pointers with no recoverable notion of
   386	$M$; checks that operate on this lowered IR --- MLIR- and IREE-style passes
   387	included --- cannot rediscover a bound the front end never materialized. The
   388	obligation was not lost in a single lowering step; it was never generated, and
   389	the information needed to generate it is erased as the program descends.
   390	
   391	The tools that might otherwise catch it sit downstream or after the fact.
   392	Unit tests exercise the sizes their author wrote, and a row count divisible by
   393	\texttt{BLOCK} never touches the boundary. Frameworks check shapes per
   394	operator on the graph being built, but the mask is kernel-internal. Runtime
   395	sanitizers catch the violation only if the offending $R$ actually runs, at
   396	device-side cost. Assertion facilities check what the programmer remembered to
   397	write. None enumerates the obligation set, and none keeps an account of what
   398	was checked and what was not.
   399	
   400	\paragraph{Our approach.}
   401	To overcome these defects, the compiler itself must generate the obligations
   402	from the constructs it elaborates, settle them while the information to do so
   403	still exists, and account for every one of them. \S\ref{sec:overview} gives
   404	the resulting workflow and then runs one kernel through it end to end.
   405	
   406	
   407	%% ----------------------------------------------------------------
   408	\section{Overview}
   409	\label{sec:overview}
   410	
   411	\sys audits a kernel in two stages, and the ordering is the design.
   412	Figure~\ref{fig:workflow} shows the workflow. At the \emph{semantic stage} the
   413	compiler first infers dimension expressions for every tensor and assigns
   414	bounded types to the iterators that parallel constructs introduce. The
   415	semantic-check pass then walks the elaborated constructs, generating the
   416	shape-compatibility, element-access, and loop-bound obligations their
   417	construct semantics imply and immediately attempting to resolve them.
   418	Generation and resolution are not separate phases: the predicate is settled
   419	while the symbolic context that would settle it is still in hand. Whatever the
   420	semantic stage cannot decide becomes a residual obligation and crosses the
   421	lowering boundary into the \emph{codegen stage}. There the target is fixed, so
   422	the hardware-constraint obligations are generated from the target's own
   423	semantics and join the residual set; each residual is then hoisted to the
   424	earliest safe placement, priced by the trip counts of its enclosing loops,
   425	and admitted or held back against a cost budget. Every obligation is thus
   426	generated from semantics --- construct or target --- and the two kinds differ
   427	only in the stage at which that semantics becomes fixed. Four terminal states
   428	result --- proven, refuted, emitted, held back --- and all four are recorded.
   429	The ledger is therefore not a report the compiler writes after the fact; it
   430	is the structure the pipeline feeds, which is what makes the accounting
   431	total.
   432	\S\ref{sec:ledger} develops the two stages; the rest of this section runs one
   433	kernel through them.
   434	
   435	\begin{figure}[t]
   436	\begin{lstlisting}[language=kern]
   437	// Symbolic M, K; concrete N = 1280.
   438	__co__ auto linear(f16 [M, K] lhs, f16 [K, 1280] rhs,
   439	                   f32 [1280] bias) {
   440	  f32 [M, 1280] output;
   441	  parallel {bm, bn} by [cdiv(M, 16), cdiv(1280, 16)]
   442	      : block {
   443	    shared f16 [16, 16] lhs_s;
   444	    shared f16 [16, 16] rhs_s;
   445	    mc = mma.fill.f32 0.0f;
   446	    foreach {k} in [cdiv(K, 16)] {
   447	      dma.copy lhs.subspan(16, 16).at(bm, k) => lhs_s;
   448	      dma.copy rhs.subspan(16, 16).at(k, bn) => rhs_s;
   449	      ma = mma.load lhs_s;
   450	      mb = mma.load rhs_s;


</details>

   463	\caption{A dynamic-shape matrix multiplication (``linear'' = matmul + bias)
   464	kernel in \sys: f16 operands with an f32 accumulator, tiled into
   465	16$\times$16 tensor-core steps on an Ampere target. The batch extent
   466	\texttt{M} and contraction extent \texttt{K} are symbolic; the output width is
   467	the literal 1280. Obligation counts in the text refer to this exact kernel.}
   468	\Description[Source listing of a matrix-multiplication kernel]{Syntax-highlighted
   469	source code of a dynamic-shape matrix-multiplication kernel: a function taking
   470	two f16 operands with symbolic batch and contraction dimensions plus an f32
   471	bias vector, containing a block-parallel loop over 16-by-16 tiles, tensor-core
   472	mma accumulation over the contraction dimension, shared-memory staging through
   473	dma copies, an mma store, and an element-wise bias epilogue.}
   474	\label{fig:running}
   475	\end{figure}
   476	
   477	Figure~\ref{fig:running} shows a matrix multiplication with two dynamic
   478	dimensions, \texttt{M} and \texttt{K}. From this source alone --- no
   479	annotations --- the compiler generates 22 safety obligations for
   480	\texttt{linear}: ten bounds checks for the \texttt{.at} element accesses, one
   481	shape-compatibility contract (the contraction extent \texttt{K} shared by
   482	\texttt{lhs} and \texttt{rhs}), three loop-bound validity checks for the
   483	\texttt{parallel} and \texttt{foreach} constructs, and eight hardware
   484	constraints imposed by the tensor-core \texttt{mma} on the Ampere target.
   485	
   486	The auditor then settles every obligation. 15 of the 22 are proven at compile
   487	time. Nine fold to boolean constants by canonical normalization --- the eight
   488	hardware constraints (each 16-wide tile dimension lies below the $2^{24}$
   489	tile-width limit the \texttt{mma} imposes) and one loop bound. The other six
   490	--- the column-index bounds on \texttt{output} and \texttt{bias} --- are
   491	proven by interval reasoning over bounded types. None is refuted: the kernel,
   492	as written, has no statically detectable defect.
   493	
   494	Seven obligations survive to runtime. Two are loop-extent checks --- the tile
   495	grid \texttt{cdiv(M,16)} is positive and the parallel extent is nonzero ---
   496	evaluated once per launch on the host (\emph{entry} cost) and admitted under
   497	the default budget. Four are the upper-bound checks
   498	\texttt{bm*16 + i < M} on the bias epilogue's element accesses into
   499	\texttt{output}; they would run device-side inside the tile loop (\emph{low}
   500	cost), and the default budget holds them back. The last is the
   501	shape-compatibility contract itself, verified host-side against the runtime
   502	shapes of the two operands. The ledger records each decision, e.g.:
   503	
   504	\begin{lstlisting}[language=json]
   505	{"loc": "linear.co:19.17",
   506	 "message": "The 1st index `((bm*16)+i)` of
   507	   element access 'output' should be
   508	   less than ::linear::M",
   509	 "outcome": "runtime",
   510	 "usage": "elem-access",
   511	 "dependence": "structural",
   512	 "cost": "low", "enabled": false}
   513	\end{lstlisting}
   514	
   515	Two properties make this an audit rather than a best-effort check. First,
   516	\emph{completeness of generation}: the obligation set is derived from the
   517	constructs, not from what a programmer remembered to assert. Second,
   518	\emph{total accounting}: the per-kernel ledger satisfies
   519	\texttt{generated} = \texttt{proven} + \texttt{refuted} + \texttt{budgeted}
   520	(here $22 = 15 + 0 + 7$), and the identity is machine-checked on a suite
   521	sample. A kernel that compiles cleanly is therefore accompanied by an
   522	explicit, inspectable safety case --- produced by the compiler, not by its
   523	(possibly artificial) author.
   524	
   525	%% ----------------------------------------------------------------
   526	\section{The Safety Ledger Discipline}
   527	\label{sec:ledger}
   528	
   529	This section presents the methodology in the order the compiler applies it:
   530	\emph{symbolic shape inference} (\S\ref{subsec:shapeinfer}) establishes, at
   531	the semantic stage, the symbolic shape expressions every obligation will
   532	quantify over; \emph{obligation generation} (\S\ref{subsec:generation}) then
   533	turns construct and target semantics into a complete set of predicates;
   534	\emph{obligation resolution} (\S\ref{subsec:resolution}) settles each
   535	predicate by evaluating the symbolic expressions it is built from;
   536	\emph{assertion materialization} (\S\ref{subsec:materialization}) turns
   537	whatever those predicates leave unresolved into budgeted runtime checks; and
   538	\emph{total accounting} (\S\ref{subsec:accounting}) records the outcome of
   539	every obligation. We begin with the inference, because obligations are
   540	predicates over the shapes it produces.
   541	
   542	\subsection{Symbolic Shape Inference over Plural Values}
   543	\label{subsec:shapeinfer}
   544	
   545	The ledger is built on a foundation the compiler already computes at the
   546	semantic stage: \emph{symbolic shape inference}. Before any lowering, \sys
   547	assigns every tensor and iterator a shape --- not a concrete extent, but a
   548	symbolic expression over the kernel's dimension symbols and the arithmetic
   549	on them. In Figure~\ref{fig:running}, \texttt{output} carries shape
   550	\texttt{[M, 1280]}, the block index \texttt{bm} carries the symbolic extent
   551	\texttt{cdiv(M,16)}, and the access \texttt{output.at(bm*16+i, bn*16+j)}
   552	resolves its row index against the inferred extent \texttt{M}. These shape
   553	expressions --- not the source syntax that produced them --- are what the
   554	obligation predicates of \S\ref{subsec:generation} quantify over.
   555	
   556	Shapes flow through a kernel in one direction, and inference follows that
   557	flow. They enter as annotations --- the parameter shapes \texttt{[M, K]} and
   558	\texttt{[K, 1280]} of Figure~\ref{fig:running} --- and are thereafter
   559	\emph{computed}, never re-asserted: a \texttt{subspan} derives a tile's shape
   560	from its parent, a chunked index derives its extent from the dimension it
   561	partitions, and the arithmetic of tiling (\texttt{cdiv}, tile-width products)
   562	produces the iterator ranges that \texttt{parallel} and \texttt{foreach}
   563	enumerate. Every tensor and every iterator therefore carries a symbolic shape
   564	expression over the same small set of dimension symbols, so the symbols that
   565	name a kernel's contract --- \texttt{M}, \texttt{K}, the grid width
   566	\texttt{cdiv(M,16)} --- recur everywhere an element is indexed or a DMA is
   567	staged. This single source of truth is what lets obligation generation
   568	(\S\ref{subsec:generation}) phrase every predicate over symbols rather than
   569	over the values of any one run, and what lets resolution
   570	(\S\ref{subsec:resolution}) decide those predicates before lowering erases the
   571	parallel structure.
   572	
   573	Inference is implemented over value numbering~\cite{briggs1997value}, with
   574	one extension that shapes force. Classic value numbering maps each expression
   575	to a single value number standing for its value; \sys instead lets one node
   576	carry \emph{plural} value numbers, one per aspect the compiler tracks --- the
   577	value itself, its implied upper bound ($\#a$), and its flattened mdspan. A
   578	tensor's shape is in turn a \emph{plural} value number: a vector of
   579	per-dimension value numbers, so a multi-dimensional shape is a single
   580	value-numbered unit that remains traversable dimension by dimension.
   581	Identical shape expressions share value numbers across sites, and the
   582	discharge engine of \S\ref{subsec:resolution} queries the resulting extents
   583	directly rather than re-deriving the expression that produced them.
   584	
   585	We claim no novelty for value numbering itself, and the inference is not
   586	where this paper's contribution lies --- an ablation in \S\ref{subsec:rq5}
   587	confirms that the sharing layer carries none of the discharge force. Inference
   588	is nonetheless the load-bearing precondition of the discipline: obligations
   589	are predicates over shapes, and the ledger needs a semantic-stage source of
   590	truth for those shapes before it can generate, discharge, or materialize
   591	anything.
   592	
   593	\subsection{Obligation Generation from Construct Semantics}
   594	\label{subsec:generation}
   595	
   596	Every kernel construct carries its own failure modes, and the compiler ---
   597	which knows what each construct means --- is the only component that can
   598	enumerate them completely. Table~\ref{tab:obligations} summarizes the mapping.
   599	Conceptually every obligation is generated from the semantics of its
   600	construct; operationally it is emitted at the stage where that semantics
   601	becomes fixed. Element-access, shape-compatibility, and loop-bound
   602	obligations are emitted during the semantic pass, alongside the shape
   603	inference that resolves their operands. Hardware-constraint obligations are
   604	emitted later, at target selection, because their semantics --- tile-width
   605	limits, alignment, atom divisibility --- is fixed by the backend rather than
   606	by the language construct. Both are obligations the compiler itself generated
   607	from semantics; neither is supplied by the programmer.
   608	
   609	\begin{table}[t]
   610	\centering
   611	\footnotesize
   612	\caption{The four obligation classes, named by the failure mode each guards,
   613	and the choreo constructs that emit them.}
   614	\label{tab:obligations}
   615	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
   616	\begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}
   617	\toprule
   618	Choreo construct & Class & Typical predicate \\
   619	\midrule
   620	\texttt{a.at(i,j)} & elem-access & $0 \le i < \mathit{dim}(a)$ \\
   621	binary ops, \texttt{dma}, \texttt{mma} & shape-compat & operand dims agree \\
   622	\texttt{parallel}\ \texttt{by}, \texttt{foreach} & loop-bound & $N > 0$, $\mathit{dim} \neq 0$ \\
   623	\texttt{dma}, \texttt{mma} (target) & hw-constraint & $\mathit{dim} < 2^{24}$, alignment \\
   624	\bottomrule
   625	\end{tabular}
   626	\end{table}
   627	
   628	Table~\ref{tab:obligations} collapses a working compiler's worth of checks
   629	into four classes, each named by the failure mode it guards. \emph{Element
   630	access} (\texttt{elem-access}) is emitted for every index of a tensor access
   631	--- \texttt{output.at(bm*16+i, bn*16+j)} in Figure~\ref{fig:running} --- so
   632	the predicate is $0 \le i < \mathit{dim}(a)$ for each index. \emph{Shape
   633	compatibility} (\texttt{shape-compat}) is emitted wherever two operands must
   634	agree: a broadcast-compatible binary operation; a DMA copy
   635	(\texttt{dma.*}, whose destination
   636	buffer must be at least as large as its source and whose transposed or padded
   637	dimensions must match); or the \texttt{mma} contraction, whose shared extent
   638	\texttt{K} couples \texttt{lhs.dim[1]} to \texttt{rhs.dim[0]}. \emph{Loop
   639	bounds} (\texttt{loop-bound}) guard iteration spaces: a
   640	\texttt{parallel}\ \texttt{by}\ N grid requires $N > 0$, and a
   641	\texttt{foreach} span requires each dimension $\neq 0$. \emph{Hardware
   642	constraints} (\texttt{hw-constraint}) are emitted at target lowering for
   643	operations whose legality the backend alone fixes --- DMA transfer dimensions
   644	$< 2^{24}$ elements and $< 2^{32}$ bytes, rank $\le 5$, padding ranges,
   645	128-byte alignment, and tensor-core tile dimensions.
   646	
   647	Because obligations are generated by the constructs themselves, the obligation
   648	set is \emph{complete by construction}: there is no path through the language
   649	or its target backends that produces an unchecked shape, bounds, or hardware
   650	hazard. We are explicit about scope: the ledger covers the failure modes of the data-movement and
   651	indexing constructs --- shape compatibility, element bounds, iteration-space
   652	validity, and target-specific limits. Concurrency safety (races, deadlock
   653	freedom) and numeric correctness are separate concerns and outside the
   654	obligation set. Within its scope, completeness stands in contrast to
   655	assertion-insertion approaches, where coverage is whatever the programmer (or
   656	generator) remembered to write.
   657	
   658	\subsection{Obligation Resolution: Evaluating Symbolic Expressions}
   659	\label{subsec:resolution}
   660	
   661	Resolution is where the ledger's predicates are settled, and it is
   662	deliberately nothing more than symbolic-expression evaluation. Every
   663	source-level obligation is a predicate over operands that the shape inference
   664	of \S\ref{subsec:shapeinfer} has already resolved, so resolution never
   665	discovers new facts --- it evaluates the predicates it is handed. Two
   666	evaluation forms cover the obligation set. A \emph{ground} predicate --- an
   667	index against a known extent --- is settled by constant folding to a boolean
   668	constant (\S\ref{subsec:canonical}). A predicate that still carries the
   669	kernel's parallel structure --- block and loop iterators over symbolic
   670	extents --- is settled by interval reasoning over the bounded types those
   671	iterators carry (\S\ref{subsec:bounded}). A third class, compiler-internal
   672	shape-consistency questions, is answered directly inside inference and never
   673	reaches the assessor at all (\S\ref{subsec:rq6}). We describe the two
   674	evaluation forms and state the soundness of each.
   675	
   676	\subsubsection{Ground Predicates: Constant Folding}
   677	\label{subsec:canonical}
   678	
   679	Obligations are generated from constructs but evaluated over inferred shapes.
   680	To check that an element access stays in bounds, the compiler does not reason
   681	about the source expression for the extent: it asks the symbolic shape
   682	inference of \S\ref{subsec:shapeinfer} for that dimension's extent and
   683	compares the index against it. The predicate handed to
   684	the assessor is already an expression over resolved values, so normalization
   685	does arithmetic rather than discovery.
   686	
   687	The normalizer folds such predicates to a canonical form by constant folding,
   688	a deterministic order on commutative terms, and structural identities
   689	($x/x \rightarrow 1$, $x{-}x \rightarrow 0$, $x \bmod x \rightarrow 0$, and
   690	gcd reduction of nested constant divisions under an overflow guard). Rules are
   691	applied by iterating reorder--fold--reassociate to a syntactic fixpoint,
   692	memoized per expression. A predicate that reaches a boolean constant is
   693	decided; one that does not stays residual.
   694	
   695	Two properties of this arrangement matter for what follows. Termination is
   696	fixpoint detection, not a rank argument: the iteration stops when the
   697	expression stops changing. And that is safe because soundness does not require
   698	the normal form to be unique --- every rule preserves semantics, so a
   699	predicate that folds to $\mathsf{true}$ holds under every valuation, while a
   700	predicate that fails to fold is not decided wrongly but passed on
   701	(\S\ref{subsec:soundness}). We therefore claim a sound decision procedure, not
   702	a confluent rewriting system.
   703	
   704	Because the operands are inferred rather than symbolic, this path decides the
   705	entire constant-only portion of the obligation set --- every predicate whose
   706	operands inference has already pinned to concrete values. Two populations
   707	dominate it: kernels whose dimensions are literals, where the fold is plain
   708	constant arithmetic, and dynamic-shape kernels in which inference had
   709	nonetheless resolved the particular extent being queried; \S\ref{subsec:rq6}
   710	quantifies the split.
   711	
   712	\subsubsection{Symbolic Predicates: Bounded Types and Intervals}
   713	\label{subsec:bounded}
   714	
   715	The one ingredient added to plain symbolic-expression evaluation is a
   716	\emph{bounded type} discipline for
   717	iterators introduced by parallel constructs:
   718	\begin{itemize}
   719	  \item $\texttt{parallel}\ p\ \texttt{by}\ N\ \texttt{:block}$ assigns
   720	    $p : \mathsf{BInt}[0, N)$ --- the block index ranges over exactly the grid
   721	    decomposition;
   722	  \item $\texttt{foreach}\ i\ \texttt{in}\ [L]$ assigns $i : \mathsf{BInt}[0, L)$;
   723	  \item chunk indices inherit bounds from the chunk algebra: for a chunked
   724	    index $p\#n$ over a dimension $D$, $\#(p\#n)$ is derived from $\#p$ and
   725	    $D$, and $\#x$ denotes the upper bound of $x$'s type. This is what makes
   726	    identities such as $D/(\#a \cdot \#b) = 1$ decidable locally.
   727	\end{itemize}
   728	To decide a predicate $P$ that normalization cannot settle, the compiler
   729	builds an interval environment $I$ mapping each free symbol to its declared
   730	range, intersected with the projection of dominating scope guards, and
   731	evaluates $P$ with interval arithmetic~\cite{cousot1977abstract}, yielding $\mathsf{true}$,
   732	$\mathsf{false}$, or $\mathsf{unknown}$.
   733	
   734	Bounded types and interval evaluation are the paper's one deliberate
   735	enhancement to the symbolic-expression evaluation of
   736	\S\ref{subsec:canonical}, and the claim made for them is a scope claim, not a
   737	novelty claim. Constant folding leaves unresolved exactly those predicates
   738	whose free symbols are iterators with declared ranges, and interval arithmetic
   739	over those declared ranges is the natural decision procedure for that residual
   740	class --- a handful of affine comparisons per obligation, never a general
   741	constraint-satisfaction query. The bounded-type discipline keeps that residual
   742	class small and its comparisons affine, so the cheap procedure meets the
   743	ledger's needs in practice, where a costlier SMT-style solver would add
   744	generality the obligation set never exercises.
   745	
   746	\subsubsection{Soundness of Discharge}
   747	\label{subsec:soundness}
   748	
   749	\begin{lemma}[Bounded-interval soundness]
   750	Let $R(x)$ be the declared range of each symbol $x$, and let $I$ be an
   751	interval environment with $I(x) \supseteq R(x)$ for all $x$. If
   752	$\textsc{EvalPredInterval}(P, I) = \mathsf{true}$, then $P(\rho)$ holds for
   753	every valuation $\rho$ with $\rho(x) \in R(x)$; if it returns $\mathsf{false}$,
   754	$\neg P(\rho)$ holds for every such $\rho$; if $\mathsf{unknown}$, no claim is
   755	made and a runtime check is materialized.
   756	\end{lemma}
   757	\begin{proof}[Proof sketch]
   758	By structural induction on $P$. The base case is the validity of declared
   759	ranges: each typing rule assigns an iterator exactly the range its construct
   760	enumerates. The inductive step is that every interval transfer function
   761	(addition, subtraction, multiplication, sign-conservative division and modulo,
   762	comparisons) over-approximates the concrete operation over the operand
   763	intervals. Guard projection is sound because dominating conditions hold on
   764	every execution reaching the check site. \end{proof}
   765	
   766	Soundness of the constant-folding path follows by congruence: every
   767	normalization rule preserves semantics, so a predicate that folds to
   768	$\mathsf{true}$ holds universally. Uniqueness of the normal form is not
   769	required --- the procedure is sound because it only ever claims a decision when
   770	it reaches a constant, and passes everything else on. We state the model's
   771	assumptions explicitly: assessment arithmetic is over mathematical integers
   772	(machine-word overflow is outside the model), and the lemma's force comes from
   773	the type discipline --- $I \supseteq R$ is guaranteed by construction of the
   774	bounded types. Both paths inherit one further assumption: the inferred shapes
   775	themselves are correct. That is shape inference's own soundness, which this
   776	paper builds on rather than re-establishes.
   777	The lemma does not cover the direct static checks inside shape inference ---
   778	compiler-internal consistency queries over already-inferred shapes, such as
   779	dimension-count agreement and span well-formedness. Those are justified by
   780	the soundness of shape inference itself rather than by interval evaluation,
   781	and their discharge is attributed separately in \S\ref{subsec:rq6}.
   782	
   783	\subsection{Cost-Gated Residual Materialization}
   784	\label{subsec:materialization}
   785	
   786	Obligations that survive static analysis become runtime checks, but under an
   787	explicit budget rather than unconditionally. A hoisting pass moves each
   788	residual assertion to the \emph{earliest} safe placement: the earliest program
   789	point that dominates the use site and follows the definitions of every symbol
   790	the predicate references, never promoted across a conditional or loop boundary
   791	(Algorithm~\ref{alg:hoist}). Three placements result: \emph{entry} (every
   792	referenced symbol is a function parameter, so the check runs once per launch,
   793	host-side), \emph{hoisted} (earlier than its use site, escaping enclosing
   794	loops), and \emph{use-site} (not hoistable). The cost at the emit site is the
   795	product of the estimated trip counts of the enclosing loops, bucketed
   796	\emph{entry} (1), \emph{low} (${<}50$), \emph{medium} (${<}500$), \emph{high}
   797	(${\ge}500$); a check is emitted only if its bucket does not exceed a
   798	configurable threshold, \emph{entry} by default --- the zero-configuration
   799	auditor costs exactly one host-side predicate evaluation per residual per
   800	launch.
   801	
   802	\begin{algorithm}[t]
   803	\Description[A formal specification of earliest-safe assertion placement and
   804	the cost gate]{A numbered mathematical specification. It first names the six
   805	relations fixed by one pre-order walk of the function body: traversal index,
   806	scope depth, enclosing block, symbol definition site, barrier set, and block
   807	trip count. It then defines the site permitted by each referenced symbol as
   808	the later of that symbol's definition site and its barriers preceding the use
   809	site, takes the latest such site over all referenced symbols, and gives two
   810	exceptions under which the assertion is immovable and stays at its use site.
   811	A while loop bubbles the site out of any scope nested deeper than the use
   812	site. A three-way case distinguishes entry, use-site, and hoisted placement.
   813	Finally the cost is the product of the trip counts of the enclosing blocks,
   814	which is bucketed and compared against the budget to decide whether the check
   815	is emitted, with the decision always recorded in the ledger.}
   816	\KwIn{residual assertion $a$ with predicate $P$ at use node $u$; budget $\theta$}
   817	\KwOut{emit node $e$, position $\varrho$, bucket $\gamma$, flag $\varepsilon$}
   818	\BlankLine
   819	\tcc{one pre-order walk of the body fixes these relations}
   820	$\omega(n)$ index of $n$;\ \ $\lambda(n)$ scope depth;\ \ $\pi(n)$ enclosing block\;
   821	$\delta(s)$ last node binding $s$;\ \ $\beta(s)$ barriers on $s$;\ \ $\mathit{anc}(e)$ blocks from $e$ to the root\;
   822	$\tau(b)$ trip count of $b$: \texttt{foreach} $\prod_{r}\lceil \mathit{span}(r)/\mathit{step}(r)\rceil$, block-level \texttt{parallel} $4$, \texttt{while} $100$, else $1$\;
   823	\BlankLine
   824	\tcc{the site each referenced symbol permits}
   825	$S \leftarrow \operatorname{sym}(P)$;\ \ $\beta^{<}(s) \leftarrow \{\, b \in \beta(s) : \omega(b) < \omega(u) \,\}$\;
   826	$\varrho_{b} \leftarrow \mathsf{in}$ if $b$ is a block $\wedge\, \lambda(u) > \lambda(b)$, else $\mathsf{aft}$\;
   827	$c_{s} \;\leftarrow\; \big(\delta(s),\mathsf{aft}\big) \sqcup \bigsqcup_{b\,\in\,\beta^{<}(s)} \big(b, \varrho_{b}\big)$\;
   828	$\mathit{imm}(P) \iff \exists s \in S:\; s \notin \operatorname{dom}\delta \;\vee\; \omega\big(\delta(s)\big) > \omega(u)$\;
   829	$(n,\varrho) \sqcup (n',\varrho')$ is the pair with the larger $\omega$\;
   830	\BlankLine
   831	\tcc{earliest safe site: the latest requirement wins}
   832	$\sigma \leftarrow (m,p) \leftarrow$
   833	$\begin{cases}
   834	  \bot & S = \emptyset\\
   835	  (u,\,\mathsf{bef}) & \mathit{imm}(P)\\
   836	  \displaystyle\bigsqcup_{s\,\in\,S} c_{s} & \text{otherwise}
   837	\end{cases}$\;
   838	\While{$p \neq \mathsf{in} \;\wedge\; \lambda(m) > \lambda(u)$}{
   839	  $m \leftarrow \pi(m)$ \tcp*{bubble out of nesting}
   840	}
   841	\BlankLine
   842	\tcc{placement, then the cost gate}
   843	\uIf{$\sigma = \bot \;\vee\; m \in \mathit{Param}$}{
   844	  $(e,\varrho) \leftarrow (\text{fn head},\ \mathsf{aft})$ \tcp*{host-side, once per launch}
   845	}
   846	\uElseIf{$m = u$}{
   847	  $(e,\varrho) \leftarrow (u,\ \mathsf{bef})$ \tcp*{not hoistable}
   848	}
   849	\Else{
   850	  $(e,\varrho) \leftarrow (m,\ \mathsf{aft})$, or the first statement of $u$'s branch when $p = \mathsf{in}$\;
   851	}
   852	$\kappa \leftarrow \prod_{b\,\in\,\mathit{anc}(e)} \tau(b)$\;
   853	$\gamma \leftarrow \mathsf{entry}$ if $\kappa{=}1$;\ $\mathsf{low}$ if $2{\le}\kappa{\le}49$;\ $\mathsf{med}$ if $50{\le}\kappa{\le}499$;\ $\mathsf{high}$ if $\kappa{\ge}500$\;
   854	$\varepsilon \leftarrow (\gamma \le \theta)$, with $\theta = \mathsf{entry}$ by default\;
   855	$\mathit{ledger} \leftarrow \mathit{ledger} \cup \{(a, e, \varrho, \gamma, \varepsilon)\}$ \tcp*{no silent drop}
   856	\caption{Earliest-safe placement and cost gating of a residual obligation.
   857	$\mathit{imm}(P)$ covers the two conservative cases --- a synthetic symbol with
   858	no definition site, and a mutable symbol redefined after $u$ --- in which the
   859	assertion is immovable.}
   860	\label{alg:hoist}
   861	\end{algorithm}
   862	
   863	The gate trades coverage for budget \emph{without} silent drops: a check above
   864	the threshold is not emitted but stays in the ledger with its bucket and
   865	\texttt{enabled=false}, so the audit records exactly what is unchecked and why
   866	(\S\ref{sec:overview}); every emitted check carries a diagnostic naming the
   867	originating construct's source location, so a runtime failure points back at
   868	the kernel source, not at generated code. \S\ref{subsec:rq3} measures the
   869	resulting overhead across budget levels. The entry-only default is host-side
   870	and loop-free by construction --- one predicate evaluation per residual per
   871	launch --- and the budget therefore trades coverage only for checks whose
   872	static trip count admits that placement.
   873	
   874	\subsection{Total Accounting: Three Outcomes, No Silent Drops}
   875	\label{subsec:accounting}
   876	
   877	Each generated obligation is settled in exactly one of three ways:
   878	\emph{proven} (discharged statically; no code emitted), \emph{refuted}
   879	(proven false; reported as a compile-time error or warning), or
   880	\emph{budgeted} (materialized as a runtime check under the cost model of
   881	\S\ref{subsec:materialization}). The compiler emits a per-kernel \ledger{}
   882	via \texttt{--dump-ledger}: one machine-readable record per obligation
   883	carrying its source location, class, outcome, information dependence
   884	(\S\ref{subsec:rq6}), deciding mechanism, and --- for runtime obligations ---
   885	its cost bucket and whether the budget admits it. The accounting identity
   886	\[ \mathit{generated} \;=\; \mathit{proven} + \mathit{refuted} + \mathit{budgeted} \]
   887	holds by construction; we verified it mechanically on a suite sample by
   888	checking that ledger records plus compiler-internal direct checks equal the
   889	assessments evaluated, for every sampled case.
   890	
   891	A refuted obligation is a bug report, not a statistic: compilation stops (or
   892	warns, for advisory classes) with the violated predicate and the source
   893	location of the originating construct. Refutation is fail-fast: when an
   894	assessment proves a predicate false, the compiler finishes the current stage
   895	--- recording the refutations it finds there --- and aborts before emitting
   896	target code, so the obligations of later stages (including any further
   897	refutations they would have produced) are never enumerated. Total accounting
   898	is therefore the property of a kernel that compiles: its ledger lists every
   899	obligation. A kernel that trips a refutation has already been shown unsafe,

   900	and the compiler stops rather than tallying the rest. A refutation is
   901	therefore always a compile-time event, never a runtime surprise: the earliest
